1. Next article in this issue Introduction Global climate challenges and the threat they pose to modern societies have become central to both scientific and public discourse over the past few decades. A critical component in finding solutions to these challenges is addressing the oversized role carbon-heavy energy sources play in our current infrastructure [1], [2], [3], [4]. Following the 2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference (COP26), further climate goals were added with the EU collectively declaring that its energy mix will comprise of 55% renewable energy sources (RES) by 2050 [5]. The United States also pledged to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 50%–52% below 2005 levels by 2030, with this target being part of a broader commitment to achieve net-zero emissions by 2050 [6]. In line with these goals, on October 13, 2023, it was announced that California would receive up to $1.2 billion from the US Department of Energy to accelerate the development and deployment of renewable hydrogen programs across the state [7]. As one of the largest residential and industrial energy markets in the world, using hydrogen to make a meaningful impact on the carbon emissions from energy and transport in the Los Angeles region requires large scale infrastructural investment [8].
